Conversely, if sleep strengthens episodic detail for the premise pairs, participants may better recognise that A and C were never encountered together and attribute A-C decisions to an indirect source. Because both outcomes are theoretically plausible, we treated source attribution for A-C trials as exploratory and tested whether subjective source ratings differed between the PM-AM and AM-PM groups.

H5. Association between premise source attribution and inference

Under a retrieval-based recombination account, A-C success should depend partly on access to the premise associations and on how successfully recognised premises are subjectively attributed. We expected higher direct-source ratings for recognised premise pairs to be associated with higher A-C accuracy, but tested whether this association was weaker in the PM-AM group (Group × Source Attribution interaction), as predicted if integration reduces reliance on premise retrieval on each trial.

H6. Dependency on premise memory

Retrieval-based recombination is strongly constrained by the availability of the premise memories. We tested whether A-C success was strongly constrained by the quantity (number of correct premises) and quality (strength of the weakest premise) of the direct associations. While we expected positive associations in both cases, we tested whether sleep reduced dependence on premise availability, predicting that the dependence of inference on specific premise availability would be reduced in the PM-AM group.

Methods

Design and overview

The study used a two-session web-based paired-associate inference task with a 12hour interval between sessions. Participants were assigned to either an AM-PM schedule (Session 1 in the morning and Session 2 in the evening) or a PM-AM schedule (Session 1 in the evening and Session 2 the following morning). Premise memory for trained pairs (A-B and B-C) was assessed at both the immediate test (T1) and the delayed test (T2). A-C inference was assessed at T2 only using novel A-C trials derived from the overlapping A-B and B-C pairs. Primary outcomes were two-alternative forced choice (2AFC) accuracy and choice RT. Secondary measures included T1 confidence ratings and T2 source ratings indicating whether each association felt directly learned or indirectly inferred.

Participants

We aimed to recruit at least N = 200 participants, with approximately n = 100 per schedule group, a target based on recruitment feasibility.

A total of N = 216 participants began the study, of whom N = 209 completed both sessions. After the exclusions described below, the main analysis sample comprised N = 167, with AM-PM n = 75 and PM-AM n = 92 (age M = 19.6 years, SD = 4.8; 126 women, 38 men, and 3 reporting another gender). Participants were recruited from the undergraduate Psychology degree programme at the University of Hull. Inclusion criteria were normal or corrected-to-normal vision, fluency in English, no self-reported neurological or psychiatric disorder, and no diagnosed sleep disorder. Participants provided electronic informed consent and received course credit. Ethical approval was granted by the University of Hull Faculty of Health Sciences Ethics Committee (Ref. 2018-PSY-14).

Materials Stimuli

Stimuli were 60 black-and-white line drawings of common objects drawn from the black-and-white line-drawing subset of the Bank of Standardized Stimuli (Brodeur et al., 2010). Objects were arranged into 20 triplets (A-B-C) by manual inspection to minimise obvious semantic or visual similarity within pairs and across triplets. This yielded 40 direct pairs (20 A-B, 20 B-C) for encoding, with each B object appearing in both pairings to enable testing of 20 novel A-C inferences.

Apparatus and platform

The experiment was implemented using the Gorilla online experimental platform (Anwyl-Irvine et al., 2020). Participation was restricted to desktop browsers. Sessions ran full-screen at participants' native resolution, and responses were collected via mouse clicks.

Questionnaires and rating scales

Participants completed a demographics questionnaire assessing birth year, gender, handedness, native language (language spoken mostly at home while growing up), vision status, and hearing status. A delay-interval questionnaire measured sleep occurrence during the 12-hour delay (yes/no, including naps), sleep duration (categorical scale from < 1 hour to 11-12 hours), bedtime and sleep onset times, number of awakenings (none to 6+ times), final wake time, sleep quality rating (6-point scale from "very badly" to "very well"), sleep problems (open text entry), and substance use during the delay period. Current sleepiness was assessed using the Stanford Sleepiness Scale [SSS; Hoddes et al. (1973)], a 7-point scale ranging from 1 ("Feeling active/vital/alert or wide awake") to 7 ("No longer fighting sleep; sleep onset soon; having dream-like thoughts").

Procedure

A schematic of the schedule and task structure is shown in Figure 1. Note. Participants encoded overlapping A-B and B-C object pairs and completed an immediate test of the trained pairs in Session 1 (T1), with confidence ratings after each response. Participants then completed either an AM-PM daytime interval or a PM-AM overnight interval before Session 2 (T2), which included delayed tests of direct A-B and B-C associations and novel A-C inference trials. T2 source ratings assessed whether participants judged each selected association as directly experienced or indirectly inferred.

Session 1 (T1)

Participants first viewed on-screen instructions, followed by a short practice block using non-experimental objects. Participants were instructed to memorise each pair by imagining a vivid interaction between the objects (e.g., "imagine the match-stick setting fire to the tube of glue"). The instructions included an example trial. Participants then completed the demographics questionnaire before beginning the encoding phase.

The encoding phase comprised 80 trials in total using spaced repetition with A-B and B-C pairs presented in alternating blocks. Block 1 presented all 20 A-B pairs (first exposure), followed by Block 2 with all 20 B-C pairs (first exposure). After a 60 s halfway break, Block 3 presented all 20 A-B pairs and Block 4 presented all 20 B-C pairs again. Within each block, trial order was randomised. On every encoding trial, the two objects were presented side by side for 6 s, preceded by a 500-ms fixation cross. The left-right positions of the two objects were counterbalanced across encoding trials. An 8 s break occurred after every 10 trials. No overt response was required during encoding, and participants received no feedback. During encoding and the immediate test, participants were not told that indirect A-C associations would later be tested.

The first session then included a 40-trial recognition test on the trained pairs (20 A-B and 20 B-C). Each trial began with a 500-ms fixation cross, followed by a cue object on the left and two choice objects on the right, one being the studied associate and the other a previously studied foil from another triplet. A objects cued A-B trials, and B objects cued B-C trials. Participants had up to 5,000 ms to choose the object they believed had been paired with the cue. They then rated their confidence on a 3-point scale (1 = "Definitely correct",2 = "Probably correct",3 = "Just guessing"), so lower values indicated higher confidence. Target position was balanced across trials, and foils were selected to minimise obvious within-triplet overlap. Participants were offered brief breaks during the test, including optional 20 s break screens. No corrective feedback was given. Participants then provided an email address to receive the Session 2 link.

12-Hour delay interval

Participants in the AM-PM group completed Session 1 in the morning and returned approximately 12 h later in the evening. Those in the PM-AM group completed Session 1 in the evening and returned approximately 12 h later the following morning. Because sessions were completed remotely, actual start times varied around the intended morning and evening schedule. The analysis therefore defined protocol adherence using the elapsed intersession delay, calculated from the first recorded choice-trial timestamp in Session 1 to the first recorded choice-trial timestamp in Session 2. Participants with an intersession delay of 18 h or longer were classified as out of protocol and excluded before behavioural quality control. The mean elapsed interval was 11.69 h (SD = 0.48, range 11.13-13.31) in AM-PM and 12.04 h (SD = 0.84, range 11.23-16.97) in PM-AM. All participants were asked to refrain from daytime sleep during the interval and to avoid excessive caffeine or alcohol. Adherence was assessed using the delay-interval questionnaire.

Session 2 (T2)

After the 12-hour delay, participants received an email link and logged in to complete Session 2. They first completed the delay-interval questionnaire about activities during the interval and then the Stanford Sleepiness Scale. Before the delayed test, participants were told for the first time that both direct and indirect association trials would appear. An example trial illustrated both the 2AFC choice and the subsequent source-memory rating on a 5-point scale.

The delayed test comprised 60 trials, including 40 direct-association trials and 20 novel indirect inference trials testing A-C relationships (see Figure 1 for an illustration of the inference structure). Each trial began with a 500-ms fixation cross, followed by the same 2AFC display as at T1 with a 5,000-ms response window. For A-C inference trials, A objects served as cues. After each choice, participants rated whether the association felt direct or indirect on a 5-point scale, with raw responses ranging from1 = "Definitely direct" to5 = "Definitely indirect". Trials were pseudorandomised to intermix direct and indirect items, and participants were offered brief breaks during the test, including optional 20 s break screens. The session concluded with a debriefing page.

Data analysis

Preprocessing and quality control Participants who did not complete both sessions were excluded before analysis. For the remaining complete cases, behavioural quality-control criteria used direct trials (A-B and B-C) only. Participants were flagged and excluded if any of the following held, with z scores computed across complete cases before behavioural exclusions and using participant-level summaries of direct trials only: (a) direct accuracy at T1 was an extreme low outlier (z < -2.5); (b) direct accuracy at T2 was an extreme low outlier (z < -2.5) with acceptable T1 accuracy (z at T1 ≥ -2.0); (c) the proportion of timeouts at T1 or T2 was an extreme high outlier (z > 2.5), where a timeout was defined as no response within 5,000 ms; or (d) mean T1 choice RT was an extreme low outlier (z < -2.5). Flags were not mutually exclusive. Trials with no choice within 5,000 ms were coded as incorrect for accuracy analyses and excluded from RT, confidence and source-rating analyses. Log1p RTs were modelled on all remaining trials, including incorrect responses.

Source ratings were reverse coded so 1 indicated "Definitely indirect" and 5 indicated "Definitely direct", with higher values indicating a stronger sense of direct source. For descriptive source-classification accuracy, direct-source responses counted as correct for A-B and B-C trials, indirect-source responses counted as correct for A-C trials, and midpoint responses counted as incorrect.

Statistical models

We used Bayesian multilevel models fitted with brms 2.22.0 (Bürkner, 2017), Cmd-StanR 0.9.0 (Gabry et al., 2025), and CmdStan 2.36.0 (Carpenter et al., 2017). Accuracy outcomes were modelled with Bernoulli likelihoods and logit links, source-rating and confidence outcomes with cumulative-logit ordinal models, and log1p-transformed choice RTs with Gaussian likelihoods. With the exception of the H3 choice-RT and H4 A-C source-rating models, models were fitted with four chains of 2,000 iterations, including 1,000 warm-up iterations, adapt_delta = 0.95, and max_treedepth = 12. Because initial diagnostics were comparatively weak for these two models, H3 was refitted with 4,000 iterations, including 2,000 warm-up iterations, and H4 with longer chains and stricter sampler controls. Sampling diagnostics were evaluated with R-hat, effective sample sizes, and divergent transitions. Focal hypothesis terms had R-hat values at or below 1.01 and adequate effective sample sizes. Weakly informative priors were used (intercepts Normal(0, 1.5); coefficients Normal(0, 0.5); standard deviations Student-t(3, 0, 2.5)). We summarise effects using posterior medians and 95% credible intervals (CrI). We interpret effects as well-supported when the 95% CrI excludes the null (log-odds 0, odds ratio 1, or .50 for chance tests). Table 1 summarises the model set, ordered by the study hypotheses. The supplement provides the full model specifications, predictor-coding details, model denominators, sampling diagnostics, and posterior predictive checks. Four further models used the same framework: a refit of the H3 RT model to correct responses only, the H1 and H2 models with centred SSS ratings added, and a model of A-C accuracy for triplets with both premises correct at T1. These are described in the supplement.

Results

Unless otherwise noted, descriptive summaries are participant-level observed means, whereas model estimates are posterior summaries from the Bayesian multilevel models.

Quality control

Of the 209 complete cases, 24 participants were flagged and excluded at the initial protocol and behavioural QC stage. Flags comprised an intersession delay of 18 h or longer (n = 12), low T1 direct accuracy (n = 4), low T2 direct accuracy despite acceptable T1 accuracy (n = 3), high T1 timeout rate (n = 6), high T2 timeout rate (n = 6), and unusually fast T1 RT (n = 0). Flags were not mutually exclusive, leaving 185 complete cases. A further 18 AM-PM participants reported sleeping during the daytime delay and were excluded from the primary AM-PM versus PM-AM comparison. The main analysis sample comprised N = 167, with AM-PM n = 75 and PM-AM n = 92. Decision timeouts were uncommon. At T1, 3.5% of direct decisions exceeded the 5,000-ms response window. At T2, 7.6% of decisions timed out across all trial types.

Alertness and adherence

Among PM-AM participants, mean delay-period sleep duration was 6.4 hours (SD = 2.1), and mean sleep quality was 4.2 (SD = 1.1) on the 6-point scale.

Descriptively, SSS ratings were similar at Session 1 (AM-PM M = 3.07, SD = 1.25; PM-AM M = 3.37, SD = 1.21; one AM-PM participant had no Session 1 rating). At Session 2, PM-AM participants, tested in the morning, reported greater sleepiness (M = 3.80, SD = 1.48) than AM-PM participants tested in the evening (M = 2.55, SD = 1.30), a difference of 1.26 points.

1. Retention of direct associations (H1)

Participant-level descriptives showed that accuracy for trained pairs was initially high but declined over the 12-hour interval, with A-B pairs generally outperforming B-C pairs (Figure 2). Descriptive means by schedule, time, and trial type are reported in Table 2. We then used the direct-pair accuracy model to test whether the amount of forgetting differed between the AM-PM and PM-AM schedules. Note. Faint lines show participant means. Solid lines and points show model-estimated accuracy. Error bars show ±1 SE. The dashed line marks chance.

Direct-pair forgetting by schedule (H1)

The observed decline in direct-pair accuracy was similar across schedules (Table 2).

Consistent with this descriptive pattern, the Group × Time contrast, averaged across A-B and B-C pairs, was centred close to no schedule difference (OR = 1.13, 95% CrI [0.86, 1.49]). On the model-estimated probability scale, forgetting was 10.9 percentage points, 95% CrI [7.8, 14.3] in the AM-PM group and 10.0 percentage points, 95% CrI [7.2, 13.1] in the PM-AM group. The model-estimated PM-AM minus AM-PM difference in forgetting was -0.9 percentage points, 95% CrI [-4.9, 2.8].

Descriptively, A-B accuracy was higher than B-C accuracy, especially at T2 (Table 2). In the model, uncertainty around the A-B versus B-C contrasts remained substantial at T1 (OR = 1.19, 95% CrI [0.82, 1.71]) and at T2 (OR = 1.33, 95% CrI [0.95, 1.84]). The schedule interaction around this pair-type contrast was also uncertain.

2. A-C Inference performance (H2-H3)

We examined whether participants inferred the novel A-C relationship above chance, and whether the PM-AM schedule was associated with higher accuracy or shorter response latency.

A-C Inference accuracy (H2)

Participant-level descriptives showed that A-C accuracy was close to chance in both schedules, as shown in Table 2 and Figure 3. The model estimates were consistent with this pattern. Across groups, the posterior predicted probability of a correct A-C response was .53 [.43, .61], with the 95% CrI spanning .50. The schedule contrast was also centred close to no PM-AM advantage (OR = 0.90, 95% CrI [0.72, 1.13]). Modelestimated probabilities were .54 [.44, .63] for the AM-PM group and .51 [.42, .60] for the PM-AM group. The model-estimated PM-AM minus AM-PM difference was -2.6 percentage points, 95% CrI [-8.2, 3.1]. The descriptive and model-based results therefore converged. Delayed A-C inference remained near chance in both schedules. Note. Violins show the participant distribution, boxplots show the median and interquartile range, points show participant means, and black point-ranges show group mean ±1 SE. The dashed line marks chance (50%).

Because the PM-AM group was sleepier at Session 2, we also refitted the H1 and H2 models with centred SSS ratings. The schedule estimates were essentially unchanged: the SSS-adjusted Group × Time estimate for direct pairs was OR = 1.05, 95% CrI [0.79, 1.38], and the adjusted schedule estimate for A-C accuracy was OR = 0.92, 95% CrI [0.72, 1.18] (see supplement).

Inferential response latency (H3)

Participant-level RT descriptives showed a graded pattern across trial types, with A-B pairs fastest, B-C pairs intermediate, and A-C pairs slowest in both groups (Figure 4). Descriptive means are reported in Table 2. The log1p RT model tested these latency differences. Positive coefficients indicate longer response times. B-C responses were slower than A-B responses (b = 0.06, 95% CrI [0.01, 0.10]), and A-C responses were slower than B-C responses (b = 0.23, 95% CrI [0.17, 0.29]). Because this model included both correct and incorrect non-timeout trials, these contrasts describe overall choice latency by trial type rather than latency for successful inference only. The schedule difference in the A-C cost relative to the average direct-pair baseline was close to zero (b = 0.00, 95% CrI [-0.04, 0.05]), giving no clear evidence that the overnight interval reduced the A-C response time cost. Note. Small points show participant geometric means. Solid lines with larger points show group geometric means. Error bars show ±1 SE. The same pattern held when the model was refitted to correct responses only. A-C responses remained slower than B-C responses (b = 0.27, 95% CrI [0.22, 0.32]), and the schedule difference in the A-C cost was close to zero (b = 0.00, 95% CrI [-0.06, 0.05]; see supplement).

3. Mechanisms of inference (H4-H6)

Because A-C accuracy was low overall, we examined trial-level measures related to A-C decisions. We asked whether the PM-AM schedule was associated with more indirect source attribution (H4) or weaker dependence on the corresponding premise memories (H5, H6).

Explicit source ratings (H4)

At T2, participants rated whether each selected association felt directly learned or indirectly inferred. Because the ratings were reverse coded for analysis, higher source-rating values indicated stronger direct-source attribution. Descriptively, source-classification accuracy for direct pairs was 65.3% for A-B and 61.7% for B-C in the AM-PM group. In the PM-AM group, source-classification accuracy was 67.6% for A-B and 63.9% for B-C. For A-C trials, the proportion correctly classified as indirect was 59.4% in the AM-PM group and 54.7% in the PM-AM group. The ordinal model provided no clear evidence of a schedule difference in A-C source attribution (OR = 1.07, 95% CrI [0.87, 1.32]).

We then examined how A-C accuracy varied with source ratings on each trial (Figure 5). A-C accuracy was highest for trials given the strongest indirect rating (Rating 1, 82.3%, SE = 1.4), lower for the adjacent indirect category (Rating 2, 60.5%, SE = 1.7), and at or below chance for the midpoint and more direct ratings. Thus, A-C accuracy was highest on trials that participants judged as clearly indirect. Note. Lines and points show group mean ±1 SE. Point size is proportional to the number of trials. Ratings range from1 = definitely indirect to5 = definitely direct. The dashed line marks chance.

Association between premise source ratings and inference (H5)

We tested whether the PM-AM schedule weakened the association between premise source ratings and A-C accuracy. For this model, higher source-rating values on direct-pair trials indicated a stronger subjective sense that the selected premise association had been directly learned. Premise source-rating predictors came from the corresponding delayed direct trials with correct responses, so this analysis tested source-rating strength among premises that were successfully recognised at T2. Premise accessibility itself is tested in the premise-count model below. Observed binned means and model estimates are shown in Figure 6. Among n = 2007 A-C trials with valid source ratings for both premises, stronger B-C direct-source ratings were associated with slightly higher A-C accuracy, although the 95% CrI included the null (OR = 1.09, 95% CrI [1.00, 1.19]). The A-B source-rating estimate was also positive, although the point estimate was smaller (OR = 1.07, 95% CrI [0.97, 1.19]). These ORs correspond to one-step increases on the five-point premise source-rating scale. Schedule moderation estimates were centred close to the null for both premise source-rating associations. Note. Source ratings for correctly recognised premises range from 1 (definitely indirect) to 5 (definitely direct). Lines show H5 model estimates at each rating of one premise, averaged over the observed ratings of the other premise, with participant and triplet effects set to zero. Shaded bands show 95% CrIs. Open points show observed A-C accuracy, with area proportional to the number of trials. The dashed line marks chance.

Dependency on premise memory (H6)

We tested whether A-C performance depended on the availability and strength of the corresponding premise memories, and whether this dependence differed by schedule. Premise count was the number of the two direct associations answered correctly at T2. Weakest premise strength combined T1 direct-pair accuracy with T1 confidence. Incorrect premise responses were scored 0, and correct responses were scored from 1 to 3 according to confidence, with higher values indicating stronger premise memory. The lower of the A-B and B-C premise scores was used as the weakest-premise score. Because direct and inferential trials were interleaved at T2, the premise-count analysis indexed an item-level association between delayed premise accessibility and A-C success, not a fixed trial-by-trial retrieval sequence.

T1 Confidence by pair type.

T1 confidence contributed to the graded premise-strength score for correct directpair responses. Because lower raw ratings indicated greater confidence, the higher B-C mean corresponds to slightly weaker confidence than A-B. Mean confidence was 1.40 (SD = 0.35) for A-B and 1.51 (SD = 0.39) for B-C. The ordinal model estimated a B-C minus A-B difference of 0.12 [0.08, 0.16] (Figure 7). Note. Bars show the proportion of definitely correct, probably correct, and just guessing ratings within each pair-type-by-accuracy cell. Premise count. Descriptively, A-C accuracy increased with the number of corresponding premises retrieved correctly at T2. The premise-count model quantified this relation, with each additional correct premise associated with higher odds of A-C success (OR = 1.49, 95% CrI [1.34, 1.69]). The schedule main-effect estimate (OR = 0.94, 95% CrI [0.75, 1.18]) and Group × Premise Count interaction (OR = 0.90, 95% CrI [0.72, 1.11]) both had 95% CrIs that included OR = 1.

Premise strength. The descriptive relation between weakest-premise T1 strength and A-C accuracy is shown in Figure 8. In the companion model, A-C accuracy increased as weakestpremise strength at T1 increased (OR = 1.22, 95% CrI [1.14, 1.30]). The Group × Premise Strength interaction estimate was centred close to the null (OR = 0.97, 95% CrI [0.85, 1.12]). Together, the premise-count and premise-strength models indicate that A-C success was related to delayed premise accessibility and to graded variation in the weaker premise. There was no clear evidence that the PM-AM schedule reduced the dependence of A-C success on premise memory. Note. Small points show participant means. Solid lines with larger points show group means. Error bars show ±1 SE. The dashed line marks chance (50%).

Inference when premise memory was strong

Because overall A-C accuracy was close to chance, we conducted two exploratory analyses of whether inference was more evident when premise memory was strong, and whether a PM-AM advantage emerged under those conditions. The first worked at the level of individual triplets, and the second at the level of participants.

Triplets with both premises correct

Successful A-C inference requires both corresponding premises, so overall A-C accuracy averages over triplets in which one or both premises had been lost. We therefore estimated A-C accuracy for triplets in which both the A-B and the B-C premise were answered correctly. For T2 premises, estimates were predictions at two correct premises from the premise-count model. For T1 premises, they came from a separate model of the qualifying A-C trials (Table 3). Note. Observed accuracy is trial-weighted. Model estimates set participant and triplet random effects to zero. T2 estimates come from the premise-count model; T1 estimates come from a model of the trials with both T1 premises correct. All 167 participants contributed qualifying trials. CrI = credible interval.

With both premises correct at T2, estimated A-C accuracy was higher than the fullsample estimate in both groups (AM-PM: .61, 95% CrI [.52, .70]; PM-AM: .58, 95% CrI [.48, .67]), and the AM-PM interval excluded chance. The schedule difference (PM-AM minus AM-PM, in percentage points) was -3.7, 95% CrI [-9.3, 1.8]. Estimates were lower when selection was based on T1 premises (AM-PM: .56, 95% CrI [.45, .66]; PM-AM: .52, 95% CrI [.41, .63]; difference -3.7, 95% CrI [-10.2, 3.4]), consistent with some premises learned at T1 being lost over the delay. A-C accuracy was therefore highest when both premises were accessible at the delayed test, with no clear evidence of an overnight advantage.

High performers

High performers were defined post hoc as participants with delayed direct accuracy of at least .75 averaged across A-B and B-C trials at T2 (n = 97). In this subgroup, the model-estimated probability of a correct A-C response was .58 [.46, .69], numerically higher than the full-sample estimate, although the 95% CrI included chance. The AM-PM estimate was .60 [.47, .71], and the PM-AM estimate was .56 [.44, .68]. The high-performer analysis therefore gave no clear evidence of an overnight advantage among participants with relatively strong premise memory.

Discussion

Across outcomes, the PM-AM schedule produced a similar pattern to the AM-PM schedule. Direct-pair accuracy declined across the intended 12-hour interval in both groups, A-C accuracy remained close to chance, and the A-C response time cost persisted. In this web-based paired-associate task, explicit inference was similar after overnight and daytime intervals when A-C relations were first tested after the delay.

The response time, source-rating, and premise-memory results were more consistent with retrieval and recombination of the studied pairs than with a newly formed direct A-C shortcut. A-C responses were slower than direct-pair responses, A-C accuracy was highest on trials judged as clearly indirect, and A-C accuracy increased with premise availability. These findings suggest that correct A-C responses were constrained by access to the studied A-B and B-C pairs, with the weaker pair acting as a bottleneck when premise memory was fragile.

Although early work reported gains after sleep in hierarchical inference (Ellenbogen et al., 2007; Werchan & Gómez, 2013) and gains after a nap in A-C or relational inference (Alger & Payne, 2016; Lau et al., 2011), later reviews and recent empirical work indicate that such benefits are small and strongly dependent on task demands and outcome measure (Cordi & Rasch, 2021; Lerner & Gluck, 2019). In a closely related visual paired-associate task, Schmidig et al. (2024) reported gains after a nap in recognition discrimination but no reliable benefit for associative cued recall. Other recent studies report null or modest sleep effects for protection from interference (Bailes et al., 2020; Pöhlchen et al., 2021), selective strengthening of future-relevant memories (Ashton & Cairney, 2021), and abstraction of regularities (Talamini et al., 2022). Links between sleep physiology and memory outcomes, such as spindlememory correlations, are also typically small and variable (Kumral et al., 2023). The present results fit this more recent pattern of small or absent sleep benefits.

Accounts of consolidation over sleep, particularly active systems consolidation, propose that recent memories can be stabilised over sleep, sometimes preferentially preserving memories that were stronger or more salient at encoding (Rasch & Born, 2013; Stickgold & Walker, 2013). In the present design, A-B accuracy was generally higher than B-C accuracy, although pair type was tied to both the materials and encoding order. The design therefore provided an opportunity to detect reduced forgetting overall or preferential preservation of the weaker B-C links. The Group × Time estimate was centred close to the null, and the A-B/B-C retention gap appeared similar across schedules. The parallel decline across groups provided no evidence that overnight sleep better preserved directly learned associations under the present remote-testing conditions.

The low A-C accuracy is consistent with retrieval-based accounts in which novel inference at test requires participants to retrieve both A-B and B-C and combine them through the shared B item (Banino et al., 2016; Zeithamova & Preston, 2010). In the present 2AFC format, identifying the correct A-C relation over a plausible lure depended on retrieving both premises and linking them through the shared B item. When access to one premise is weak, performance can approach chance even when average premise accuracy is above chance. This view aligns with the observed increase in A-C success with the number of correctly recognised premises, and it helps explain why any modest preservation of premise memories may not have been sufficient to support novel inference reliably.

The exploratory analyses of strong premise memory further characterised this premise-dependent pattern. For triplets in which both relevant premises were recognised at T2, A-C accuracy was higher than in the full sample and exceeded chance in the AM-PM group. Among participants with strong delayed premise memory (≥ .75 accuracy on A-B/B-C at T2), A-C accuracy was numerically higher, although its interval included chance. Both analyses showed similar performance across schedules, extending the main result beyond the full-sample average.

The encoding schedule may have limited inference. A-B and B-C pairs were presented in separate blocks (A-B, B-C, A-B, B-C) rather than fully interleaved trial by trial. Some accounts propose that integration is promoted when encountering an overlapping item (B) triggers retrieval of its earlier associate (A) during encoding of the new associate (C), creating an opportunity for online coactivation and tagging of overlap for later offline processing (Schlichting & Preston, 2015; Shohamy & Wagner, 2008). This blocked structure reduced opportunities for rapid switching between overlapping episodes and may have limited the online coactivation thought to support later integration. The encoding schedule therefore represents an important boundary condition for interpreting the null sleep effect. Future work could test this directly by manipulating trial-level interleaving.

Source ratings showed modest differentiation between selected associations on direct and indirect trials. Because participants were told at T2 that indirect trials would appear, these ratings are best interpreted as subjective judgements about how they made each choice, rather than as direct evidence that an A-C relation had been stored as a new memory. Source ratings were similar across schedules. A-C accuracy was highest on trials rated as clearly indirect, whereas accuracy was at or below chance when participants endorsed more direct source ratings. On successful A-C trials, participants tended to judge the decision as an inference supported by the premises rather than as direct memory for an A-C association. However, recognising an association as indirect did not guarantee correct responding in a 2AFC task, where both options may be familiar. This dissociation between structural awareness and item-level discrimination is consistent with source-monitoring and dual-process accounts in which the subjective basis of a response can diverge from the recollective evidence needed to resolve specific alternatives (Johnson et al., 1993; Yonelinas, 2002).

The sleep manipulation was limited by the standard AM-PM versus PM-AM schedule. Sleep opportunity was confounded with time of day and retrieval state, and the PM-AM group also reported greater sleepiness at Session 2. Sleep during the delay was assessed by self-report rather than objective monitoring such as actigraphy or polysomnography. Some AM-PM participants reported interval sleep and were excluded from the primary comparison, which reduced the analysed sample and highlights the difficulty of maintaining a clean daytime-wake contrast in a remote design. These features limit conclusions about specific sleep physiology.

The task also set a high threshold for detecting small schedule-related effects. The web-based setting introduced variation in testing environment and response timing, which may have reduced sensitivity to subtle effects on choice RT or inference accuracy. Presenting A-C trials only at T2 avoided immediate inference testing and reduced test-induced learning, but it also meant that there was no baseline measure of A-C performance before the delay. Thus, the design assessed delayed explicit inference rather than change in A-C performance across the interval. The immediate test of the premise pairs may also have reduced the scope for a sleep benefit on direct-pair retention, because sleep can enhance recall of restudied material while leaving recall of previously retrieved material unaffected (Abel et al., 2019; Bäuml et al., 2014). Low overall A-C accuracy also leaves uncertainty about sensitivity to a small sleep benefit. This concern does not by itself explain the null effect, because laboratory nap studies have also reported limited sleep benefits for related visual paired-associate outcomes (Schmidig et al., 2024).

Future work should test these boundary conditions with designs that separate schedule effects from sleep more cleanly and that lower uncertainty about the mechanism. One priority is to manipulate premise strength and encoding structure directly, for example by using criterion learning for A-B and B-C and by comparing trial-level interleaving with separate A-B and B-C blocks. A second priority is to vary the timing of the inference test by comparing T2-only A-C testing with designs that include an immediate A-C baseline, so that offline change in inference can be separated from delayed inference level. Interval structure could also be controlled more tightly with nap versus wake designs, counterbalanced schedules, or longer delays that include multiple nights of sleep. Objective sleep measurement, whether through actigraphy in home settings or polysomnography in the laboratory, would strengthen manipulation validation and allow links to specific sleep features. Targeted memory reactivation during slow-wave sleep could then test whether externally cued replay is sufficient to improve A-C inference when premise traces are weak to moderate (Hu et al., 2020; Santamaria et al., 2024).

In conclusion, the present findings provide no clear evidence that the overnight PM-AM schedule improved retention of directly learned associations or explicit A-C inference in this paired-associate task. Instead, A-C success was constrained by the accessibility of the studied premise memories and was strongest when participants experienced their choices as indirect inferences. These findings suggest that sleeprelated benefits for paired-associate inference, when they occur, may depend on conditions that make premise memories robust and overlapping relations salient.

Yonelinas, A. P. (2002). The nature of recollection and familiarity: A review of 30 years of research. Journal of Memory and Language, 46(3), 441-517. https://doi.org/10.1006/jmla.2002.2864

Zeithamova, D., Dominick, A. L., & Preston, A. R. (2012). Hippocampal and ventral medial prefrontal activation during retrieval-mediated learning supports novel inference. Neuron, 75(1), 168-179. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2012.05.010
